Vinita, you asked whether forgiving Radhika means you have to be friends again. Let me, Imran, tell you about Salim. At twenty-five we opened a small printing shop in Aligarh together. People said, "He's your childhood friend, forgive him." And in the end I did forgive him, slowly, over years. I stopped wishing him harm. When his mother died, I went to the funeral. But I never went into business with him again, and I never lent him money again. Forgiving was for my own heart. Trust was a separate thing, and he had used up his chances. So forgive Radhika if you can, when you're ready. That doesn't mean you owe her your secrets again.

Ganga had prepared for the call with Shreya from HR the way she used to prepare for exams: a page of notes on her desk in the Bengaluru flat. Three projects she had led. What two friends in similar roles had said about pay. A range she could live with.

I was scared of another fight like that one. And, honestly, I was too proud to say sorry first.

At the Sri Rama Navami fair in Chaitanya's village, Lalit anna played the harmonium on the temple steps, fingers flying, and the whole crowd clapped along. Chaitanya, nine, forgot all about his cotton candy.

He played the same three notes over and over, grinning. After school he played again. After dinner he played on the veranda until the neighbour's dog howled.

Third week of the monsoon holidays in Bishnupur, and Anirban's tabla practice had gone grey. The first days had been thrilling: new bols, Baba recording him on the old phone, Thakuma clapping from the veranda. Now it was the same teen-taal, round and round, and his fingers still stumbled on the same fast phrase. Ustadji said it would take months.
From the lane came Jeevan's whistle. "Anirban! Fishing at the big pond, everyone's going!"
Anirban looked at the tabla, then at the rain-washed lane. One day off won't matter, he thought. I practised yesterday. I'll do double tomorrow.
He didn't feel like practising at all. Nothing of the early excitement was left to pull him to the mat. He sat there, half up and half down, listening to Jeevan's footsteps slow outside the gate.

His brother Daniel, home from university, glanced up from the sofa. "Going out in that?"
Lars pulled on one shoe and stopped. It's just rain, he told himself. Then another voice: skip today, the plan says rest days are fine anyway. It didn't, actually.
He sat on the bottom stair, one shoe on and the other in his hand, feeling nothing like the boy who had started this. Daniel said nothing, just raised an eyebrow.
Lars tied the second lace slowly, not because he wanted to, but because he'd said he would.

It doesn't seem mean. He looks proud when I say “thank you”. Shalini told him, “You're always telling!” and moved away from him at snack time.
I now ask, “Is anyone hurt? If not, tell your friend directly.” I will also make Ojas water-bottle helper, so he has another way to feel useful.

In Dadi's courtyard in the village, Alka came running. “Dadi! Bhavna is on the guava tree! You said no climbing!”
Dadi looked up from the grain she was cleaning. Bhavna's legs were dangling from a branch.
Alka stood very straight. “I told you first. I'm good, na?”
“Bhavna, come down slowly,” Dadi called. Bhavna climbed down, glaring at Alka. “Tattle-tale!”
Alka's smile wobbled. She looked at Dadi, then at Bhavna.
Dadi pulled Alka into her lap. “Thank you for telling me about the tree. It's high. Next time, you can also tell Bhavna first. She's your friend.”

In the kindergarten in Osaka, Dalia marched up to Ms Tanaka before snack time. "Yuki took two crackers. And Ren splashed at the sink. And Hana didn't wash her hands." She stood very straight, waiting.
Ms Tanaka knelt. "Thank you, Dalia. Is anyone hurt or in danger?"
Dalia thought. "No."
"Then let's see if Yuki wants to share the extra cracker."
Dalia looked back at Yuki, who was holding both crackers and frowning at her. Dalia's smile wobbled.

I'm Anirban, and Class 9 was the year I became invisible.
Nobody bullied me. That's the strange part. No one was mean. I just wasn't invited. Birthday photos appeared online, and I wasn't in them. Group projects formed before I even looked up. When the class went for ice cream after the exam, they'd already left by the time I packed my bag. Nobody waited.
After a few months, I started thinking there must be something wrong with me. My voice, my face, my jokes. I stopped talking in class.
Then, in Class 10, Kavitha asked me to be in her science group. Just like that. I remember how shocked I was. I remember thinking, oh. Maybe it wasn't me.

School counsellor's note, Class 10, Thursday.
Ganga came to see me today, very quietly. She is not being bullied, she said. “Nobody does anything to me. That's the problem. I'm just not there for them.”
She described the class group chat where plans are made she's never tagged in, a birthday picnic she saw only in photos, and the walk to the bus stop where her old friend Keerthana now walks ahead with others.
Then she asked, “Is something wrong with me? Maybe I'm boring.”
I told her clearly that being left out does not mean something is wrong with her. We talked about one club she might join. I'll meet her again Monday, and with her permission, I'll speak to her class teacher about mixing groups for projects.

Saturday night
The party photos are up. Everybody from our class was at Tanya's. Everyone except me and maybe two others. I didn't even know there was a party.
It's not like anyone was mean. That's what makes it worse. Nobody told me not to come. They just didn't think of me.
Even Jeevan, who sits with me in tuition, didn't wait after class on Friday. He just walked off with the others, laughing.
I keep looking in the mirror. Is it my face? Do I talk too much? Too little? There must be something.
Mumma asked why I skipped dinner. I said headache. I don't know how to explain being nobody's first thought.
Sarika

I'm Vaibhav, and last year I was editor of the Class 10 magazine. I thought I'd be brilliant at it.
On day one, I made a chart. Ojas: sports page. Meena: poems. Kartik: cover. I decided the theme myself, the colours, even the fonts. I told everyone the deadline twice a day. When Ojas suggested an interview with the canteen uncle, I said, “No time. Just do the sports page.”
It felt efficient. But by week two, nobody spoke in meetings. They sent their pages late without a word. Ojas said, “It's your magazine anyway,” and walked off.

Robotics club mentor's note, Week 4.
Harsha was chosen as team captain this term. He is hard-working and very sure of himself. He has assigned every task, changed the design twice without asking, and rewrites other members' code at night.
He believes this is the fastest way. But the team has gone silent. In today's session, when I asked for ideas, nobody spoke. Kalyani, one of our best programmers, has missed two sessions. She told me, “There's no point, he'll change it anyway.”
When I mentioned this to Harsha, he looked genuinely hurt. “I'm doing everything for them.”
I will run the next session as a planning meeting where each member proposes one feature, and talk with Harsha about asking before changing.

"Friday. Done. One hundred percent," Elizabeth said at Monday's stand-up in their Bengaluru office, tapping the table. The new payment screen was hers, and she said it like a fact. Two teammates exchanged looks; last month her "two days" had become nine. Salma, the team lead, didn't roll her eyes, and she didn't just write Friday on the board either. "Great," she said. "Give me a range. Best case, likely case, and what happens if the bank's test server goes down again?" Elizabeth paused. "Best, Friday. Likely... Tuesday. Worst, if the server dies, the Friday after." Salma wrote all three on the board and told the client Tuesday. Elizabeth looked relieved.

"I'll have the whole audit report by Wednesday," Manjula said. "Easy."
"The whole thing?" Rakesh asked. "Forty branches?"
"I've done audits before. Trust me."
Rakesh leaned back. "Last time you said that, we missed the deadline and the client—"
"Rakesh," said Devika, the senior analyst, gently. Then to Manjula: "I believe you're fast. Help me plan it, though. If everything goes right, when?"
"Wednesday."
"And if two branches send their data late, like in March?"
Manjula frowned. "Then... Friday. Maybe Monday."
"What's the worst case?"
"If the northern branches don't reply at all, a week late."
"Okay," Devika said. "I'll tell the client Friday and flag the northern branches today."

Looking back at my first year as a team lead, the thing I'm proudest of is how I handled Kashish. I'm Avni, and I inherited a developer who promised everything with total confidence. "Two days, boss. Guaranteed." The first time, I believed him and told the client. It took eight. The second time I was so annoyed I nearly took the task away from him in front of everyone. Instead I tried something my old manager used to do: "I trust you're good. Give me three numbers: best case, likely, and if something breaks." He laughed, then thought. "Two, five, ten." It came in at six. We still use the three numbers, and now he gives them before I ask.

Supervisor's note, Rao Caterers kitchen, Tuesday. Written by Aparna.
Akash, our newest cook, told the team this morning that he would have all 600 sweet boxes for the Kulkarni wedding ready by Thursday noon. "No problem at all, I've done big orders." He said it with such certainty that two of the helpers started planning their Thursday off. I've seen this before, so I didn't argue and I didn't just nod. I asked him, "What's your best case, your likely case and your worst case?" He paused. Best: Wednesday night. Likely: Thursday morning. Worst: if the khoa delivery is late, Friday. Friday is too late for the wedding. So we ordered the khoa a day earlier and kept one helper on standby for Thursday. Akash looked a bit deflated at first, but later he told me, "I never think about the worst case. That was useful."

Wednesday night
Today at the NGO meeting, Gopika said she'd translate the whole 80-page health-worker manual into Marathi by Monday. "Easy, I'm fast," she said, and everyone relaxed. I'm the team lead, and part of me wanted to believe her, because the funders are waiting. Another part of me remembered how long the last 20 pages took. I didn't want to embarrass her in front of everyone, and I didn't want to just say okay either. After the meeting I asked her over chai, "What's the fastest you could do it, what's realistic, and what could go wrong?" She laughed first, then thought. Fastest Monday. Realistic Thursday. Worst, if the medical terms need checking, two weeks. Two weeks! So I've told the funders "next Friday" and asked Rohini to check terms in parallel. Gopika seemed relieved, actually.

In the hospital's IT department in Shillong, Sunaina said in the Monday meeting, "The new billing system will be ready by Friday. Hundred percent." She sounded completely sure. You asked how I manage Lakshmi on my team? Honestly, she's brilliant, but she's certain about everything. "Two days, guaranteed." "No risk at all." At first, I believed her. Then the warehouse software took three weeks instead of two days, and the client was furious. I was so angry I almost stopped giving her big tasks. That wouldn't have been fair either. So now, whenever she says "guaranteed," I smile and ask, "Best case? Worst case?" At first she found it annoying. Then she started answering properly: "Best case, two days. Worst, ten, if the supplier's data is messy." Now I plan around the worst case and cheer the best. The client's happy. Lakshmi's happy. And I sleep. That's how Zubin learned to work with a colleague who's always sure.

Jeevan, you want the honest version? Fine. I'm Anirban, and for eighteen months I kept a game project alive that everyone, including me, knew was dying. Twelve downloads. Twelve! But shutting it down meant saying out loud that a year and a half was gone, and that hurt more than anything. Meanwhile I had a small design job for a local bakery that was actually paying and growing. The moment it made decent money I quit, because I was scared it would fall apart and I wanted to leave on a win. So I dropped the winner and kept feeding the loser. In June he bought forty copies of an old-syllabus exam guide cheap, sure they'd sell. They didn't. The syllabus had changed. Every week I say, "Let's sell them to the raddi shop and use the shelf." January. With her first internship stipend, Usha, twenty-one, bought small amounts of two companies' shares. Within a month, one had risen 8% and one had fallen 12%. She sold the rising one straight away. "Lock it in before it drops," she told her friend Vishal. The falling one she kept. "It'll come back. If I sell, I've lost."
April. The one she sold had gone up another 20%. The one she held had fallen further. Usha stopped opening the app. She felt sick every time she thought of it, so she didn't think of it.
July. Vishal asked, "If you had this money in cash today, would you buy that company?" Usha was quiet. "No." "Then why are you holding it?" "Because selling means admitting I lost." She said it out loud and it sounded strange even to her. That night she wrote down what she'd learned before doing anything else.

It was the Rath Yatra fair in Puri, the crowd like a river. A boy in a yellow shirt knocked my sugar candy right into the mud. I decided he was the wickedest boy in Odisha. I told my mother and cousins. Then, ten minutes later, the crowd surged, and I fell against an old man and knocked his glasses off. I said, "It was the crowd, it wasn't me!" My mother just looked at me. "And the boy in yellow?" she asked. I had no answer. So, Hrithik, was the corridor crowded when he shoved you?

On the first rainy day of July, everyone crowded into the school corridor because the ground was a lake. I was holding my wet umbrella when Suraj banged into me and my tiffin fell open. "You're so mean!" I shouted. He said sorry, but I didn't listen. I told everyone Suraj was a bully. Ten minutes later I got squashed near the staircase, and my elbow hit a small girl from class two. She started crying. "It wasn't my fault," I told the teacher. "It's so crowded!" At home I told Mummy about both bumps. She raised her eyebrows. "Sumit, so which one of you is mean?" I didn't have an answer. I still don't, really.

By her third month at the university in Nairobi, Vera had started keeping a quiet tally. The campus café hired students for evening shifts, and the manager, a cheerful man from Nyeri, always seemed to have "just filled" the slots, usually with students who had grown up near Nyeri too. Her flatmate Wanjiru had applied twice, with more free hours than anyone. Both times she got a polite no.
In the lab it was similar: the senior student helped the two from his old school first, then looked round for the rest.
One evening Vera said it plainly. "Wanjiru, I don't think it's you. I think it's where people come from."
Wanjiru was quiet, then nodded slowly. "I thought I was imagining it."
Next morning they listed the dates she had applied and asked the student welfare office how café shifts were meant to be given out.

In Block C of the girls' hostel, the warden's office opened at eight for room-change requests. Jasleen, second year, sat on the bench outside with her form and noticed something she couldn't unsee. The senior who helped the warden sort requests was from Ludhiana, and so were the three girls who walked out smiling with better rooms. Rajni, from a small town in Jharkhand, had been waiting since October for a room away from the leaking wall. Her form was still "under process".
That night in the mess, Jasleen, who was from Ludhiana herself, admitted it aloud to Rajni. "I got my room in a day. I didn't ask why. I think I know now."
Rajni put down her spoon. "So it wasn't my form."
The next morning Jasleen went back with her and asked the warden to show the waiting list in date order.

Swati, you asked why I started that hostel help-desk thing. Okay. I'm Neelam, second year, and it began with me just noticing.
In our hostel, when a senior had spare notes, they went to juniors from their own state. When the canteen needed a student helper, the manager hired "someone from back home." When a first-year from Manipur asked for help with the library portal, three people said they were busy, and ten minutes later helped a boy from their own district.
Nobody was being cruel. It just flowed that way, like water downhill. I did it too. My first instinct was always to help people who spoke my language.
So I started saying it out loud at hostel meetings: "Help keeps going to our own people." People were uncomfortable. But then we set up a shared notes shelf and a sign-up sheet for helpers, open to anyone. Last week the Manipur girl ran it.

I'm Kiran, and I share a lab bench with Salma in our engineering college in Coimbatore. This semester I watched her do something unusual.
Our department's paid research assistant posts went to six students. Salma pulled up the list on her phone one evening and showed me. All six had gone to the same two schools as the professors. Then she scrolled back: last year, the same.
She didn't sound angry, more puzzled. "The professors probably trust people who remind them of their own school," she said. "Maybe they don't even know they're doing it."
I don't know how nervous she was, but her hands were tight around her phone when she asked the head of department, politely, if the posts could have a written application and a scoring sheet next time.
He frowned, then said he'd think about it. This month's notice board has a form on it. Salma just pointed at it and smiled.

Wednesday. When an interesting client project came up, the manager gave it to the newest intern, from his old college, who had joined a week after Sunaina. Sunaina got the filing. Nilesh watched her face tighten, then smooth over.

Sachin, you're starting hostel life next month, so let your Kishore mama tell you something I saw at twenty. In our engineering hostel, the mess committee was run by boys from one district, and I was one of them. Every good room, every late-night pass, every second helping of paneer went to our own. The relief camp had only forty blankets left, rain was still pouring, and the queue stretched to the road. In my first year at the Pune hostel, I did something I only noticed later. I'm Jahnavi, from Nagpur, and I found the other Marathi-speaking girls on day one. We shared pickles, notes, and scooter rides. When one of us needed a lab partner, we asked each other. In second year, I put my notes on the common drive for everyone, and I told my friends why. Naming it felt awkward. Not naming it had been worse.

Mess committee note, Block C hostel, written by Anand, secretary.
Three part-time helper slots opened this month. All three went to students from our own district. I signed the list myself.
Lalit, a second-year from the north-east wing, had applied first, and his form was complete. When I asked the committee why he was skipped, one member said, "We know our boys, they're reliable." I nodded along at the time.
Once I started looking, I saw it at the counter too: second helpings of dal go faster to people who speak our language.
What I did next: I put Lalit's name back on the list and asked the committee to agree on simple rules (first to apply, full form, rotation every month) and pin them on the notice board.
Saying this out loud at the meeting was awkward. Keeping quiet felt worse.

Kunal, you know I started tutoring the first-year stats batch? I'm Vandana, by the way, in case your podcast needs names.

For three days after the half-yearly results in their village near Bikaner, Sarita, fifteen, told everyone the maths marks weren't out yet. The report card sat folded in the wheat bin under the stairs. Twenty-eight out of eighty. Each night her stomach knotted. On the fourth evening, on the rooftop, her cousin Himani didi was folding clothes. Sarita's mouth felt dry. "Didi. I got twenty-eight in maths." Saying it out loud made her chest shake, and then, oddly, loosen. Himani didi put down the clothes. "Okay. Thank you for telling me." She didn't scold. They sat on the parapet and made a small plan: ask Masterji for the paper tomorrow, one chapter a day, and tell Amma on Sunday together. Sarita took the report card out of the wheat bin that night.

"Horrible. Also... a tiny bit lighter."
"That's the point. Now, what's one thing you can do this week?"
"Ask Ma'am what went wrong in the essay."
"Good. And telling Ammi?"
"Tonight. Will you sit with me?"
"Of course."

When the chemistry marks came out in March, I told everyone I had done "fine". I even told myself the paper must have been marked wrong. For two weeks I did not open the answer sheet; it lived folded inside my geometry box. Then my cousin Madhuri asked about it at dinner, and something in me stopped pretending. Afterwards I showed her the sheet on the terrace. "Thirty-one out of eighty," I said out loud. Saying the number hurt, but less than hiding it had. Madhuri did not lecture. She just asked, "Okay, Gautam. Which chapters?" We circled three together. That night I wrote a weekly revision list and stuck it above my desk.

Vishal, you asked how I got my weekends back. Okay. For two years my head said the same thing every Sunday: you should be more productive. You should be learning to code, you should be at the gym, you should be doing the side project. I'd say it and then lie on the bed feeling guilty and doing nothing. Then my cousin said something annoying and true: 'Rajni, nobody runs towards a should.' So I tried an experiment. Instead of 'I should be productive', I said, 'I'd like to get a bit more done, and today that means one thing: thirty minutes on the side project after lunch.' That's it. One sentence, one step. It felt almost too small. But I did the thirty minutes. Then I did it the next Sunday. The guilt got quieter. I finished the thing in March.

I'm Shankar. Madhav and I have shared a flat in Pune for two years, so I know his Sunday routine: a groan, then 'I should clean, I should study for the CA exam, I should call home,' and then four hours on his phone. Last month something changed, and I'm only guessing at why. He stuck a small card on the fridge. It said: 'I'd like to be on top of things. Today: one chapter, before lunch.' Just that. That Sunday he read one chapter at the dining table with his earphones in. The next week the card said: 'I'd like the flat to feel nice. Today: the kitchen only.' He did the kitchen. I don't know what's going on inside his head, but the groan has stopped, and last Sunday he asked if I wanted chai while he worked.

Monday: Lakshmi's to-do list at the bank branch began with 'I MUST be more productive', underlined twice. By four she had answered emails, reorganised her drawer and done none of the loan files. She felt sick with guilt on the bus home.
Thursday: Sangeeta, at the next desk, glanced at the list. 'Must? Who's shouting at you?' Lakshmi laughed, then didn't. That night she rewrote the top line: 'I'd like to clear the Kulkarni file. First step: check the land papers, 10 a.m.'
The next Monday: By 10:40 the land papers were checked. The Kulkarni file went to the manager before lunch. Lakshmi crossed it out, wrote the next wish and its first step, and noticed she wasn't underlining anything any more.

The sticky note on Sharmila's laptop in their Salt Lake flat said, in her own capitals, I SHOULD BE MORE PRODUCTIVE. It had been there for three weeks. Every Sunday she looked at it, felt a wave of guilt, and then somehow spent the afternoon sorting her phone photos. Today Anjali, passing with a bucket of wet clothes, tapped it. 'Does that ever work?' 'No,' Sharmila admitted. 'It just makes me feel like rubbish.' She peeled it off. On a fresh note she wrote: I'd like to get the research report moving. Step: write the introduction for 25 minutes after lunch. It looked almost too small. After lunch she set a timer. At the ring she had three paragraphs, rough but real, and she kept going for another twenty minutes without deciding to. She stuck the new note where the old one had been.

For most of last year I lived with a sentence in my head: I should be practising more. I'm a junior sound engineer, and the violin was meant to be my other life. Every evening I'd come home, see the case by the door, and feel the should land on my chest like a sandbag. Then I'd scroll on my phone until midnight, guilty the whole time. It was Hafsa, my old music teacher, who asked on a call, 'What if you said "I'd like to" instead, and picked one thing?' It sounded too soft to work. But I tried it: I'd like to play again, so I'll do ten minutes of scales before dinner. The first night, ten minutes became thirty. Not every night since, but most. The violin case stays open now. The sandbag is lighter, and Nikita the violinist feels like a real person again.

Salma ran the only computer training centre in Tarikere, and every night she wrote the same line in her notebook: "I should be more productive." Every night she felt guilty, because the line was never crossed off. There were fees to collect, a new course to design, a leaking roof to fix. One evening Ujjwal, who taught the typing class, saw the notebook. "Every day the same sentence?" She nodded, ashamed. "It makes me feel lazy before I even start." He picked up a pencil. "What if it said: 'I'd like to be more productive. Tomorrow's one step is...' " She thought. "...call the roofer." He wrote it down. The next morning she called the roofer before the first class. It took four minutes. That night she wrote, "I'd like to finish the course outline. One step: write the first two lessons." She did those too.

On the fridge of Paulo's flat in Porto was a sticky note in his own capital letters: I SHOULD WRITE MY THESIS. It had been there for three weeks. Every time he opened the fridge for milk, his stomach sank. Yara, his flatmate, took it down one Sunday morning and held it up. "How does this make you feel?" "Like a failure. Every morning." Paulo rolled his eyes but wrote it. It felt different. Softer. Possible. He wrote one page before lunch, then another after. Two weeks later, the fridge had a row of small notes, each one with a tick. The old one was in the bin.

At the district office, Lalit's desk was buried in pending files. At the Monday team meeting, he said, "I should be clearing more files. I should be faster. I'm the slowest in this section." Saurabh, the section head, noticed he said "should" three times. After the meeting, Saurabh stopped by. "How do those shoulds help?" Lalit laughed weakly. "They don't. I just feel guilty all weekend and then do nothing on Monday." Saurabh took a blank sheet. "Say it as something you'd like, and add one step for today." Lalit thought. "I'd like to clear the pension files by Friday. Today, I'll finish the five oldest." "Good. That's a plan, not a punishment." That afternoon Lalit finished the five oldest files and put a sticky note on the sixth. On Friday, the pension pile was gone. The guilt, he noticed, was lighter than it had been in months.

Dear Ramesh,
Remember the list you saw on my fridge in January? "I should wake at 5. I should write 1,000 words a day. I should stop being so lazy." By March I'd done none of it, and I felt guilty every time I opened the fridge for milk.
So I tried what you suggested at the tea shop. I rewrote it: "I'd like to write more. Today: 200 words before breakfast." It sounds tiny, I know. But the next morning I actually sat down and wrote 340, because the goal didn't feel like a punishment.
It's been three weeks. Some days only 50 words, but I haven't missed one. The guilt is mostly gone, and the story is finally moving.
Thank you for the tea and the idea.
Pema

Parul, sit, let me tell you something since you're starting college. When I was a young man of twenty, I got a letter saying I'd won a scooter in a lucky draw. Just pay a small "delivery fee" by money order, it said. I sent almost my whole month's hostel money. No scooter came, of course. When I told my elder brother, he laughed and said, "Harish, you're a fool. Who wins scooters? You deserved to lose it." I didn't eat properly for days, not from hunger but from shame. It was my roommate Jacob who sat with me, said, "They're thieves, you're not stupid," and walked with me to the post office and the police chowki to report it. The money never came back. But the shame slowly left.

Student counsellor's note, by Amandeep
Sudha, second-year B.Com, came in today, very low. Last week she paid a "registration fee" for an online internship that turned out to be fake. The company's page vanished. When she told her flatmates, one said, "How could you be so stupid? Everyone knows those are scams." Her cousin said, "You should have been smarter." She said to me, "I feel like an idiot. I lost the money and now I feel ashamed too."
I told her plainly: the people who built the fake page chose to cheat her. That is on them. Scams are designed to fool careful people.
What I did next: we sat together and she called her bank's helpline from my office to report it, and we filed a complaint on the national cyber-crime portal. I've asked her to come back on Monday, and I gave her the number of the college's legal aid cell.

Thursday night
I got scammed. A man called saying he was from a courier company, that my parcel was stuck and I had to pay a small fee to release it. I clicked the link and entered my card details. Two withdrawals followed within minutes.
I told Bidisha, and the first thing she said was, "Bhavna, how could you fall for that? You're supposed to be smart." I felt my face burn. I went to the bathroom and cried. It wasn't just the money. It was feeling stupid on top of it.
An hour later Bidisha knocked. "I'm sorry. That was a horrible thing to say. You didn't do this, they did." Then she sat with me while I called the bank to block the card, and we filed a complaint on the cyber-crime helpline. The bank man said they see hundreds of these a week.
I still feel shaky. But not stupid any more.
Bhavna

Programme coordinator's note, leadership workshop, day 3.
During the peer-feedback hour, most participants picked colleagues who would be gentle. Tushar walked straight to Nandini, who had worked under him for a year, and asked, “What am I missing about how the team sees me? Please don't soften it.”
I was close enough to hear. Nandini hesitated, then said that the team found him approachable, but that he often changed deadlines late at night by message, and people felt they could never switch off.
Tushar's jaw tightened. He started to say, “That's only when clients…” and then stopped himself. He wrote it down. He asked her what one change would help most.
Afterwards he told the group he had been sure his team saw him as flexible.
Next step: I suggested he check in with Nandini again in a month to see whether the late-night messages have reduced, and I added the example (with both their consent) to our session notes on feedback.

Thursday.
Asked Swati tonight the question I've been dodging for months. Why do my flatmates keep moving out? Three in two years. My story has always been: bad luck, fussy people.
We were on her terrace with cold coffee. I said, “Be honest. What am I not seeing?”
She put her glass down. “Okay. You're generous and fun. But you decide things for the flat without asking. The wifi plan. The cleaner's timings. The new sofa. People feel like guests in their own home.”
My first reaction was to feel attacked. The second, about ten minutes later on the walk home, was: oh. The sofa. I did do that.
It's embarrassing to hear. It's worse to keep losing people and not know why.
Tomorrow I'm going to ask Ishani, before she decides to move out too, what she'd like a say in.
— Noor

After a year as the new teacher at the government school in Bhiwani, Nikita thought she was doing well. Her results were good. But fewer parents came to her meetings each month. One evening, walking back past the mustard fields with Salma, the senior teacher, Nikita asked, "Salma ji, tell me honestly. What am I missing with the parents?" Wanjiru had pitched her solar-lamp startup to four investors in Nairobi and been turned down four times. Each time she told herself they just didn't understand the market. Before the fifth pitch, she called Bao, who had run two companies and never softened anything. "Bao, watch my pitch and tell me what I'm not seeing. Don't be nice." She ran through the slides on a video call. Bao was quiet for a moment. "You rush past the sales slide. You say 'early traction' and click away. Investors notice. It looks like you're hiding something." Wanjiru felt her jaw tighten. "The numbers are small." "Then say they're small, and say why. Hiding them is worse than small." She sat back. She had rushed that slide in every pitch, and never once noticed. "Thank you," she said. "That's exactly the thing I couldn't see." At the fifth pitch, she stayed on the sales slide for two full minutes.

After the Thursday planning meeting at the Hyderabad office, Rashmi caught Sachin by the photocopier. "Five minutes? I need you to be rude to me."
He laughed. "Rude?"
"Honest. I think that meeting went well. I think I'm a good listener. But everyone went very quiet, and I don't fully trust my own opinion of myself. How did I really come across?"
Sachin hesitated, then said it. "You asked for ideas, and then you finished Neelam's sentences three times. By the third time she stopped talking. And you said 'great point' right before saying why it wouldn't work."
Rashmi's ears went pink. She wanted to explain, and didn't. She wrote down: finishes sentences; "great point, but". "Thank you. Will you tell me if I do it again next week?"
"Only if you promise not to sulk."
"I'll sulk quietly," she said, and smiled.

By 6 a.m. Jaya was on the terrace of her Hyderabad hostel with her guitar, playing the same four bars over and over until they sounded right. By 9 a.m. she was in the electronics lab, yawning over a circuit board. She had chosen engineering with her eyes open: her father's shop had nearly closed twice, and she wanted a job that paid on time. She didn't regret it, exactly. Her friend Gautam noticed the difference, though. "In lab you look like you're waiting for a bus," he said. "On the terrace you look like you forgot the time." Jaya laughed, but it stung a little. That evening she did her circuit homework properly, then played for an hour, and didn't feel guilty about either.

'You're playing again at midnight?' Nandini Apa stood in the doorway in her nightclothes.
Farida lowered the harmonium lid. 'Just ten minutes. I finished the thermodynamics sheet first.'
'Ammi says you're wasting engineering fees.'
'I'm not wasting anything. I chose engineering, Apa. I want a salary. I never want to borrow the way Abbu did.' Farida rubbed her eyes. 'But when I sing, it's the only time I'm not counting.'
'Counting what?'
'Marks. Money. Years.' She looked at the harmonium. 'In class I do my best, but it's like pushing a cart. Here, the cart pushes me.'
Nandini came in and sat on the bed. 'So do both. Just don't sing at midnight before a lab exam, okay?'
Farida smiled for the first time that evening. 'Okay.'

I'm Karthik, and I'm in the third year of civil engineering. I picked it for two reasons: my uncle said the jobs are steady, and I liked the idea of never worrying about rent. Both reasons are still true. But on Friday nights I play bass for Sabina's college band in the canteen, and I come alive in a way I never do in surveying class. Last month Sabina asked if I'd join their tour of three small towns during the winter break. I wanted to say yes so badly my hands shook. I also had an internship interview that week. I went to the interview. I got it. On the bus home I listened to our band's recording twice, and I still don't know if I chose right. Maybe it's both.

Faculty mentor's note, second-year electronics. Wasim.
Suraj came for his mid-semester review today. Marks fine, attendance fine, but he says he 'drags himself' to lab. When I asked why electronics, he was honest: 'My father's shop closed two years ago. I want a job that doesn't disappear.' That's a real reason, and I told him so.
Then I mentioned I'd heard him on tabla at the cultural night. His whole face changed. He talked for ten minutes about a rhythm cycle he's composing, hands tapping the desk.
He isn't choosing wrongly; he's pulled two ways, and the pull is draining him.
Next steps: I suggested the audio signal processing elective next term and told him about the college's recording room. We'll meet again after exams to see where his energy goes.

Thursday
Surveying practical in the sun from nine to one. I measured the wrong baseline twice and Rubina had to redo it. My head was full of the tune I started on Monday.
Here's the thing I don't say out loud: I'm not sorry I took civil. Abbu's salary won't stretch forever, the placement numbers are good, and I like knowing I'll be able to pay Ammi's rent one day. That's real. That matters to me.
But tonight I finished the second verse on Rubina's old keyboard, and it felt as if the whole day had been waiting for that one hour.
Rubina said, 'Sehar, you're two different people. One of them is very sleepy.'
She's right. I don't know how to be both yet. Maybe I don't have to pick. Maybe I just have to stop pretending one of them doesn't exist.

In Satara, Aditi cycled every morning to the government engineering college, where her seat in civil engineering had made her father stand a little taller at the milk dairy. A government job someday, a pension, a house with a proper roof: she wanted those things too, honestly. But on Thursday evenings, in the temple courtyard, she sang abhangs with the village women, and her voice filled the whole square. Her cousin Eshaan, home from Pune, heard her one Thursday. 'You don't sound like that about bridges,' he said. Aditi laughed, then stopped. In class she took neat notes and forgot them by evening. At the temple she remembered every line after one hearing. 'I like bridges,' she said slowly. 'I love this.' Eshaan waited. 'Both are true,' she said. 'I just don't know how much of me each one gets.'

In Kraków, Marek's computer science lectures started at eight, and he made them every day. He had chosen the course for good reasons: his parents had struggled after the factory closed, and programmers were hired everywhere. He wanted that security, badly. But in the evenings he played violin in the flat's narrow corridor, because the sound was good there, and his flatmate Soo-ah stopped whatever she was doing to listen. 'You look different when you play,' she told him one night. 'Different how?' 'Awake.' Marek laughed it off, but he knew what she meant. His code assignments got done, correctly and on time, like chores. The violin got his real attention, the kind where he forgot to eat. Both mattered. He just noticed that one of them had to be pushed, and the other pulled him.

At 11 p.m., the engineering hostel's common room was empty except for Hansa and an old harmonium someone had left behind.
Her roommate Indu found her there. “Thermodynamics quiz tomorrow, Hansa.”
“I know. I've done it. Mostly.” Hansa's fingers kept moving over the keys, soft, so the warden wouldn't hear.
“You never play like this in the lab,” Indu said.
Hansa laughed. “The lab pays the rent later. This…” She stopped. “Papa says engineers never go hungry. He's right. I want that too. A steady salary, a flat for Amma.”
“And this?”
“This is the only hour of my day I don't check the clock.”
Indu sat down. “Both can be true, you know.”
Hansa nodded slowly. She did her circuits homework on time but with half her heart. The harmonium got the other half.

So you're asking why I'm always half-asleep in mechanics, Baljeet. Fair question.
I'm Vikas. I picked mechanical engineering because my uncle said it's safe. Placements, a good salary, maybe a job abroad. And I want that. I watched my parents count every rupee. That's not nothing.
But here's the secret: every night I'm on the terrace with my guitar, writing songs until one o'clock. I've written twenty-three this year. I've written maybe two good lab reports.
When I'm writing a song, I forget to eat. When I'm doing a lab report, I check my phone every five minutes.
I'm not going to drop out. I also can't pretend the music is a hobby. Both of these are me. I just haven't figured out how to give each one a proper place yet.

First week of July. Irfan filled in the form for mechanical engineering. "Good jobs, good salary," he told his roommate Ujjwal. "Abbu will stop worrying." In his cupboard, under his shirts, was the old guitar he'd played since Class 8.
Five weeks later. Irfan sat through thermodynamics half asleep. He'd been up till two, playing guitar on the hostel roof. In the lab, he did the minimum. Ujjwal asked, "Do you even like engineering?" "I like what it'll give me," Irfan said. "Security. That matters." "And the guitar?" "That's just for me."
Three months later. Irfan joined the college band, two evenings a week. On other days he studied properly, even stayed back for a machine design doubt. "Engineering pays the rent," he said. "Music keeps me awake enough to do it." Both reasons were true, and he'd stopped pretending either one wasn't.

Yusuf, you want to know why I became a nurse when I loved singing? Let your Hansa Nani tell you. I was nineteen. My father had just lost his job at the mill. Nursing meant a stipend, a hostel, a job waiting at the end. Singing meant nothing but hope. So I chose nursing, and I told everyone it was my calling. But in the first year I was so tired and flat. Christmas week in Bengaluru, and the hostel was half empty. Elizabeth sat on her bed with the thermodynamics textbook open at the same page for forty minutes, while down the corridor someone played carols on a cheap keyboard, badly.
She knew exactly why she had chosen engineering. Her father had said it on the phone again that morning: steady jobs, good salary, no begging for gigs. And she agreed with him. She did. She wanted a flat of her own one day and never to worry about rent.
But when Vaibhav knocked and said, "The guy next door is murdering 'Silent Night', come fix it," she was off the bed before he finished. For two hours she sat at the keyboard, rearranging chords and teaching Vaibhav a harmony, never once checking the time.
Back in her room, the textbook was still on page 212. She sighed and started reading.

Arnab had worked at the same bank branch in Salt Lake for three years. His manager was kind, the pay was fine, and he could process a loan file with his eyes shut. That was the problem. He had started scrolling through his phone between customers, forgetting what he'd scrolled. At home he snapped at nothing. "You're not sick, you're bored," said Kalyani from the next counter, when he sighed for the fourth time before lunch. Then she mentioned that the branch was starting a money-basics workshop for small shopkeepers and needed someone to design the sessions. Arnab volunteered that afternoon. For the next month he stayed late making charts and practising in front of the mirror. He was tired. He felt more awake than he had in a year.

Anders had worked the IT help desk at the insurance company in Gothenburg for three years. Reset passwords, fix printers, replace mice. He could do it half asleep, and lately he nearly was. Sonali's mushroom shed behind the house in Ambegaon had failed by September. The spawn had gone mouldy in the damp heat, and the few kilos that grew rotted before the Tuesday market. The neighbours whispered. For two days she didn't go out. On the third, she sat on the verandah with a school notebook and wrote down, plainly, what had happened: shed too hot, no fan; spawn bought from an unknown seller; no buyer fixed before harvest. She read it to her husband Saurabh and to Kulkarni madam at the farm advice centre. Madam nodded. "Every farmer here has a notebook like this in their head. Most never write it down." In November Sonali tried again: a cooler corner of the cowshed, spawn from the centre, and a hotel in town that agreed to buy every week.

Emeka's phone-repair kiosk in the Leeds shopping arcade closed on a wet Tuesday in February, eight months after it opened. He carried the last box of screen protectors to his car and sat there, engine off, for a long time.
That night he opened a notebook and wrote a heading: WHAT THE KIOSK TAUGHT ME. Rent too high for the foot traffic. Too many cheap cases, not enough repairs. Tuesdays dead, Saturdays mad. Customers came back because I was honest about what wasn't worth fixing.
On Sunday he read the list aloud to Liam, his flatmate, and to his older sister on a video call. Liam added one line: 'You were great with students.'
In June Emeka started again: no rent, a repair van outside the university twice a week, repairs only. The first Saturday he fixed eleven phones. He stuck the old list inside the van door.

Aadhya slid the notebook back. "Can I be your first test customer? A real stranger price." 😊

My name is Shankar, and two years ago the flood took my plant nursery on the edge of Guwahati. It was not only the water. I had rented low land because it was cheap, ordered too many saplings at once, and never kept records of what sold.
For a month I told everyone it was bad luck. Then Nirmala, who had lent me the first pots, asked me a plain question over tea: "What would you do differently?" I went home and wrote it all down. Four pages.
I read them to Nirmala and to my uncle, nobody else. It felt like undressing in public.
The new nursery sits on higher ground. I grow in small batches and write every sale in a register. When a plant dies now, I just add a line.

People ask how I got so much done this quarter. Honestly, I stopped waiting for good days and started building them. I'm Deepika, I write code for a logistics firm, and until March my mornings were chaos: chat pings, email, "quick calls". Then my lead, Farida, sat with me and we made three rules. English teacher's note, Class 10-B — Payal
Jeevan did not submit his essay on Monday. When I asked, he showed me his notebook. The first page had been written, rubbed out and rewritten so many times that the paper had torn through in two places.
He said he had started the introduction nine times. "It wasn't good enough, ma'am. The first sentence has to be perfect or the whole essay is bad." He looked exhausted, and his hands were grey with eraser dust.
He is one of my strongest writers. I think he has set the bar so high that completing anything feels like losing.
I told him that a finished, imperfect draft gets more from me than a perfect sentence that never arrives. I asked for a rough draft by Wednesday, in pen, no erasing allowed, and promised feedback, not a grade.
He brought it on Wednesday. It was good.

Coach's note, college hockey squad, for my own records.
Swati, our right half, showed me her phone today. After three months, Soo-ah, her roommate, saw it too: the blue dots, for tearful and flat, came in the same few days before Yara's period; the gold ones, for energetic, came about a week after. "You had your big exam in a blue week last term," Soo-ah said. Yara groaned, remembering crying in the library over a missing highlighter. This term she booked her swim time-trial for a gold week and marked the blue days for gentle walks, early nights and easy reading. When the blue days came, she still felt low. But she didn't call herself lazy. She made tea, pulled on thick socks, and let herself rest.

In the university biology lab, Deepika kept a small notebook next to her pipettes. Kiran, her lab partner, finally asked what she was always jotting down. 'My cycle and my moods,' Deepika said, unembarrassed. 'Three months of notes. See? Around these days I'm tired and tear up at nothing. Around these, I'm sharp and can manage long runs.' She had moved the fiddly cell counts to her strong week and kept the low days for reading papers and leaving on time. 'I used to think I was just weak on those days,' she said. 'Now it's just planning.' Kiran looked at the neat columns. 'That's smarter than my to-do list.'

On the terrace of their Indore apartment block, Yash was crying so hard after an argument at home that he couldn't stop gulping air. Soon his fingers tingled and the water tanks seemed to tilt. "Something's wrong," he gasped. "I'm going to pass out." The fear made him breathe even faster. Dhruv, his neighbour from the fourth floor, sat down beside him. "You're breathing really fast. That can make you dizzy. Try breathing out slowly, like you're blowing through a straw. Longer out than in." Yash tried. Out, long. In, short. Out again. After a few minutes the tingling faded and the tanks stood still. He wiped his face. "That was scary." "Yeah. If it keeps happening or worries you, tell your mum and see the doctor, okay?" Yash nodded.

"Coach, my hands are fizzing. My lips too."
"Harish, look at me. How are you breathing?"
"Fast. I can't get enough air. The final's in ten minutes. What if I faint on the track?"
"You're getting plenty of air. Too much, actually. Fast breathing can make you tingly and light-headed. It feels scary, but slowing down usually eases it. Sit on the bench."
"Okay."
"Now breathe in through your nose, short. Out through your mouth, long, like you're fogging a window. Longer."
"Like this?"
"Exactly. Again. Keep the out-breath long."
"The fizzing's going."
"Good. You were breathing like a sprinter before the race even started."
Harish laughed shakily. "Yogesh Sir, don't tell the others."
"Your secret's safe. But if this keeps happening, we'll talk to your parents about a check-up."

Jasleen read the message from her best friend three times, then started crying so hard she couldn't stop. In the small bedroom of their Chandigarh flat, her breaths came quicker and quicker, gasp after gasp. Her fingers began to tingle. The room swayed. Now she was scared as well as heartbroken. 'Something's wrong with me,' she sobbed. Her older brother Kunal came in and sat beside her. 'Jasleen, you're breathing too fast. That's what's making you dizzy. Breathe out with me, long. 'If this keeps happening, we'll tell Mummy and see the doctor,' Kunal said.

The night after Nana's funeral, I cried harder than I'd ever cried in my life. Sitting on the terrace, I couldn't catch my breath. I was gulping and gulping, and then my lips went numb and my hands started tingling. I thought, I'm dying too. That made it so much worse. My cousin Nilesh found me there. He didn't panic. He sat close and said, 'Zubin, you're breathing too fast. Your body's getting too much air. Breathe out with me, really slowly.' He breathed out long and loud, like a sigh, and I copied him. After a few minutes the tingling faded. I was still crying, but I wasn't scared any more. Later that night Nilesh told my mother, and she sat with me until I slept. I still think of that terrace when grief hits hard.

Sports teacher's note, inter-school meet, Saturday
After losing his 400-metre heat by a fraction, Raghav sat behind the stands crying hard and breathing very fast. Within a minute he said his fingers were tingling and his head was spinning, which frightened him more, and he breathed even faster. I knelt beside him, kept my voice slow and said, 'Breathe in gently, and out even longer, like blowing on hot soup.' I breathed with him. After a few minutes the tingling faded and he could sit up. He was embarrassed, so we talked privately. I've told his parents and suggested he mention it to his doctor at his next visit.
Tanuja

"So wait," Harsha said. "Yesterday you said Aman asked for your number."
"He did. Well, he asked for the notes. And then he said 'thanks' in a really specific way."
"At lunch you told the girls he asked you to the fest."
Indu's ears went pink. "He said 'see you at the fest'. That's basically asking."
"And you told Mehr he wrote you a note."
"I didn't say note. I said message."
"You said note. With a heart."
Indu slumped against the locker. "Okay. It keeps getting bigger. Every time someone goes 'ooh', I add a bit. Now I can't remember which version I told who."
"Sounds exhausting."
"It is. And if Aman hears any of it, I'll die."
"So stop adding. Just say he's nice and you like him."
"That's so boring."
"It's also true," Harsha said.

Dear Kalyani,
Remember how I kept calling my job "just a rough patch"? Last night I tried the trick you taught me. I pretended you had told me the same story: your boss takes credit for your work, you haven't learned anything new in a year, and you cry on Sunday nights. I knew straight away what I'd say to you: "That isn't a rough patch. That's a pattern."
Then I asked myself the second question: what would change my mind? If my boss credited me even once in the next month, maybe. I wrote that on a sticky note and put it on the fridge.
I didn't shout at myself or call myself stupid. I just looked at it. It was oddly calm.
With love,
Baljeet

Mehak, you asked how I finally decided about the bakery. For three years I said, "Next season it'll pick up." Every season I said it. Then one night, instead of arguing with myself, I asked a kinder question: "Farhan, if your best friend told you this, what would you say?" I knew the answer. I'd say, "Show me the numbers, yaar."
So I asked the second question: what would actually change my mind? I wrote it down. If orders don't grow by Diwali, I stop and look for a job. No drama, no calling myself a failure.
Diwali came. They didn't grow. It hurt, but it didn't feel like defeat. It felt like I had finally been honest with someone I care about. Me.

She wrote for a while. Then she read aloud: "I'd ask, does he say good morning to anyone? Has he said anything about my work directly?"
She looked up at me. "He doesn't say good morning to anyone. And he gave that client to Rohit because Rohit speaks Tamil." She laughed a little. "What would change my mind? If he actually criticised my work."
On Monday, she asked the manager for feedback. I don't know exactly what he said. But she came home humming.

The bus was stuck in Gurgaon traffic for forty minutes, and Simran's phone was at six percent. She kept replaying her manager's words from the meeting: "Sloppy again." It was the fourth time this month.

Vinita replied fast: "If I told you my boss said 'sloppy' four times without ever saying what was wrong, what would you tell me?"

Then one monsoon he whispered a different secret: he was going to swim across the swollen river to prove he wasn't a coward. My chest went tight. That night I told my father. The school bus was stuck in rain-soaked traffic, windows fogged, forty children shouting songs. Rashmi leaned close so only Simran could hear. "We're moving to Chennai after the exams. Don't tell anyone. I want to tell them myself."
Simran nodded hard, feeling proud and heavy at the same time. For the rest of the ride she hugged her bag and said nothing when a boy asked why Rashmi looked sad.
She thought of what Mummy always said: secrets that make someone unsafe must go to a grown-up. This one wasn't unsafe. It was just sad, and it was Rashmi's to tell.
When the bus finally reached school, Simran squeezed Rashmi's hand. "Tell me when you're ready to tell them. I'll sit next to you."

Class 4 teacher's note, Friday.
At break, Dolma whispered a secret to Rekha. I don't know what it was, only that Rekha came back glowing, standing a little taller.
By the last period she looked worried. Two girls kept asking, "What did Dolma tell you?" Rekha pressed her lips together and shook her head.
After class she came to my desk. "Ma'am, do you have to keep a secret forever?"
I told her most secrets, like a surprise or a crush, are for keeping, and that's what good friends do. But a secret about someone being hurt or in danger must go to a grown-up, and that isn't betrayal.
She thought hard, then smiled. "Mine's a keeping one." She skipped off to find Dolma.

On my second month as a trainee at the architecture firm, I sent the site the wrong ceiling height for a classroom. Three hundred millimetres too low. I found it myself on a Friday evening, rechecking a drawing for something else.
Nobody else had noticed. The contractor would start the framing on Monday.
I sat in the empty office for twenty minutes, sweating. I could quietly send a "revised" drawing and hope nobody compared. But if the framing started wrong, children would sit in that room for years.
I called Lakshmi Ma'am, my supervisor, at home.
She was sharp. "How did this happen? Did you not check against the section?" I said no, I hadn't, and that it was my mistake. She was quiet, then: "Send the corrected drawing now, copy me. I'll call the contractor."
It stung for a week. But since then, she gives me the checking of other people's drawings.
Chaitanya

Course note, Physics Lab II. Written by Uday, faculty in charge, 18 February.
Tanvir, one of our final-year teaching assistants, came to my office at 8 a.m. today, before class. He looked as if he hadn't slept.
He told me that while grading the circuits quiz last night, he had used last year's answer key for the first twenty papers. Six students had lost marks they had earned. No student had complained yet; the marks were not published.
I was not gentle. I told him that grading is a trust we give assistants, and that he should have checked the key before starting. He did not argue or make excuses. He said, "You're right. I've already regraded all twenty with the correct key. The changes are in this sheet."
Next steps: marks published correctly this afternoon. I have told Tanvir that I will continue to give him grading duties. Frankly, I trust him more after this morning, not less.

Café manager's note, Saturday
At 3:40 pm Indu, our weekend trainee, came to me pale and said, "I wrote the wrong label on Mrs D'Souza's birthday cake. I wrote 'nut-free', but I used the almond sponge. The pickup is at five."
Nobody else had noticed. Abhinav, on the counter, had already boxed it.
I'll be honest: I scolded her. I said allergy labels are the one thing we can never get wrong. She nodded and didn't argue, though her hands were shaking.
Then we fixed it. I called Mrs D'Souza, explained, and offered a fresh nut-free cake by six or a full refund. Indu and I baked the new sponge together. It went out at 5:55.
What I did next: I added a second check for allergy labels on every order, and I told Indu that because she spoke up, nobody got hurt. From next month I'm putting her in charge of the allergy checklist.

Monday
I told Pradeep sir today. My legs were jelly.

I could have said nothing. He'd have blamed the old computer.

He shouted. Properly shouted. "Do you know what three weeks means?" I stood there with my face burning.
Then he rubbed his eyes and said, "Fine. The lab assistant has a USB copy from Thursday. We'll lose one day, not three weeks. Next time, ask before deleting anything."
This evening he asked me to help him re-enter Friday's data. He said, "You told me before I found out. I'll remember that."

We were at Samir's birthday, six of us boys around a table of kebabs, when Samir told a joke about a woman who couldn't park. Everyone laughed. Then he told another one, a rape joke, the kind I'd heard a hundred times and laughed at to fit in.
This time I didn't laugh. I thought of my sister, who takes two buses home after dark.
The table went quiet around me. Samir said, "Arre, Kishore, it's a joke, yaar."
I kept my voice easy. "I know, bhai. It's just not funny to me. Tell the one about your driving test instead."
For a second nobody moved. Then someone laughed, at Samir's driving test, and the talk moved on.
Later, one of the others texted me: "Glad you said that. I didn't know how." Looking back, that text mattered more than the silence.

He pulled her file over, stamped two pages and said, "Come Friday for the copy."

Thursday night. I left my bag in an auto this morning with my exam hall ticket in it. I nearly cried at the school gate. At lunchtime the guard called me out: the driver, Samir Bhai, had come all the way back, and he put the bag in my hands. I tried to give him money. He folded my fingers over it and said, "Beta, study well. That's my payment." Then he was gone before I could thank him properly.
I've been thinking about it all evening. My worries about tomorrow's paper feel smaller, like somebody opened a window. I want to be a person who does that for someone one day.

Last winter, in an ordinary physics class, Usha Ma'am said something I still can't shake. She was explaining the elements, and she stopped and said, "The iron in your blood was made inside a star that exploded billions of years ago."
The room went quiet. I looked at my hands. My hands were made of old stars. I'd been so anxious that week about a fight with my best friend, replaying every word. Suddenly it felt tiny, and I felt huge and tiny at the same time.
After class I asked Ma'am how anyone had figured that out. She lent me a book. I read it on the bus, twice. I'm Zeenat, and whenever I'm stuck in my own head now, I look at my hands.

Music teacher's note, Friday.
Our choir visited the old age home today. An elderly resident, Mrs Fernandes, asked to sing for us: a hymn from her childhood, in a thin, shaking voice. Nobody moved.
I watched Sharmila, who usually jokes through everything. She stood completely still, hands pressed together, eyes wet. On the bus back she didn't touch her phone. When I asked if she was all right, she said, "I don't even believe in much, Ma'am. But it felt like the whole room lifted."
She asked if the choir could learn the hymn to sing with Mrs Fernandes next month. I said yes.
Jahnavi

Tuesday
Today was supposed to be terrible. Maths test, and I'd fought with Mummy about the phone, and I was angry all the way to the bus stop.
Then something happened. There was a little boy at the stop, shivering, no sweater, crying because he'd missed his school bus. An old lady, Kalyani Aunty from the flower stall, came out, took off her own shawl, wrapped him in it, and gave him a glass of hot tea from her flask. She didn't even know him. She waited with him until his father came.
I just stood there. Something in my chest went huge and quiet. Like when I heard the gurdwara kirtan at dawn last Gurpurab. My maths test, the fight, everything, suddenly looked so small.
In the test I was calm. I think I did okay.
Tonight I said sorry to Mummy.
Jasleen

At Sangeeta's birthday party last month, I got stuck near the balcony with her, talking about the college canteen. It was a nice chat, but I was starving and the biryani was disappearing.
Earlier that evening, I'd seen someone just walk away from a person mid-sentence, and that person looked hurt. I didn't want to do that.
So when there was a natural pause, I smiled and said, "It was really great talking about this. I'm off to get some food before it's gone. Catch you later?" Sangeeta laughed. "Go, go! Save me a kebab." I brought her one, actually.

Later I saw them sitting together at breakfast. I'll mention this at the next freshers' talk as a good example of ending a conversation well.

Every Sunday at four, my grandfather Omkar tells me about the day the river flooded his village. I'm Deepak, and I've heard it maybe thirty times: the boat, the tin trunk, his mother carrying the brass lamp. When I was fifteen I would say, "Nana, I know." Now I just settle into the chair and ask something new.
Last week I asked, "What did the water smell like?" He stopped, looked at the ceiling, and said, "Like wet earth and burnt wood." He had never said that before. His eyes went shiny. I think he wants the story remembered more than he wants me to learn from it.

I watch from my balcony, since Yamini's family sits in the courtyard below every evening. Yamini's Nani was on the cot, telling the flood story again. I've heard it drift up at least four times. She always starts: "That year the river came at midnight."
Yamini, elbows on knees, listened as if it were new. Then Yamini asked, "Nani, who carried the little ones?" The old woman lit up, her hands started moving, and she named two names I'd not heard before.
Yamini didn't correct or hurry her. When it was done Nani patted Yamini's cheek. I don't know what Yamini thinks each time, but Yamini never looks bored.
— Zainab, neighbour

Dadu looked at both of them for a long moment. "No. But you two know his name now."
Afterward, Bhupen wrote the name, Pobitro, and the smell of mud and diesel in a notebook he'd started keeping.

Looking back, I'm a bit embarrassed. I'm Imran, and in Class 6 everybody was playing that football card game, the one with the shiny player cards. Yusuf had forty cards. The whole back bench traded at lunch.
I didn't even like football much; I liked drawing. But when twenty kids all want the same thing, it feels like they must know something you don't. So I saved my pocket money for three weeks and bought two packs. I traded for exactly one lunch break. Then the craze moved to marbles.

At every family gathering in the village that winter, someone said it: the new mobile tower behind the temple was making the cows give less milk. Chacha said it at the engagement. At the Thursday board-game nights in Lars's flat in Gothenburg, the old windows always came up. The panes were wavy, thicker at the bottom, and every few weeks someone explained why: glass is secretly a slow liquid, and over a hundred years it had sagged. Daniel said it first, back in the autumn. Then Maja repeated it to a new guest, and by spring it was simply part of the flat, like the creaky floor. Lars had nodded along a dozen times. One evening, while Daniel was telling it again to his cousin, Lars asked, "Where did we first hear that? I honestly can't remember." Daniel grinned. "A teacher, maybe? A quiz show?" Nobody was sure. Lars looked it up while the dice rolled: old glass was often made uneven, and builders tended to set the thick edge at the bottom. "Huh," said Daniel. "Better story, actually."

Chinmay, you asked what happened at Sunday lunch. It's about the mall. You know how our old college gang meets every month and someone always says the new mall by the metro is built on a graveyard, and that's why the escalators stop? Mridula's cousin said it first, I think, two years ago. Then everyone started saying it. Last Sunday Ravi told the new guy, Abel, very seriously, and everyone nodded like it was a known fact. I'd nodded for two years. So I just asked, friendly, "Wait, where did we first hear that?" Silence. Then laughter. Nobody knew. Ravi said, "I just... know it?" and then, "Huh." Abel looked relieved, honestly. I wasn't trying to catch anyone. I just realised we'd all been passing one story around the table until it felt like history.
That's it. That's my big Sunday. Thomas out.

I'm Chinmay, and I've watched this happen at every family lunch for two years.
Someone says, "You know the lake behind the colony is being filled for a mall." My cousin Sharmila nods. Masi adds, "Next year, they say." Everyone sighs. Nobody asks anything. It has been said so many times it's like the weather.
Last Sunday, Sharmila said it to her in-laws with total certainty. I watched their faces go worried.
Later, while we cleared plates, I asked her quietly, "Where did we first hear about the mall, do you remember?"
She paused, spoon in hand. "Masi, I think? No... that forward in the family group, ages ago."
"Did it ever say who?"
She laughed, a bit embarrassed. "No." She put down the spoon. "Why do I keep saying it, then?"
Nobody was scolded. Next lunch, nobody mentioned the mall.

Friday. At the usual dinner at Kavitha's, someone said the new supermarket on the main road sells last week's vegetables in fresh bags. Everyone agreed. Santosh noticed he'd heard it from Kavitha the week before too.
The following Friday. Same group, same table. The supermarket came up again. "Old vegetables, everyone knows," a friend said. Kavitha added a story about a soft tomato. Santosh watched the claim get bigger each time, and nobody asked a single question.
Three weeks later. When it came up again, Santosh put down his fork. "Honest question. Where did we first hear this? I've forgotten."
Silence. Kavitha thought. "My neighbour said it, I think. She'd heard it from someone."
"Has anyone actually bought vegetables there?"
Nobody had. Kavitha laughed. "Okay. That's a bit silly." Santosh laughed too, and passed the rice.

Rashmi, beta, at every wedding when I was your age, somebody told the same story: that our family came from a palace in Rajasthan and lost it all in a card game. Your Nani's sister, Kamla Masi, told it best, with her hands flying.
I was twenty-five. I'd heard it maybe thirty times. I noticed nobody ever asked a question; people just nodded, like at a prayer.
One Diwali, I sat by Kamla Masi and asked, "Masi, where did we first hear this? Who told you?"
She stopped. I was scared I'd hurt her. But she thought, and said, "My father's cousin. He liked stories."

She laughed until she cried. I'm Ujjwal, beta, and she still told the palace story, but now she started it with "Our Chacha used to say..."

I went back to Thrissur in April after four years in Dubai. I'm Vinita, and I had been dreaming of that town the whole time: the temple pond, the old library, the ice-cream cart outside Shankar Uncle's shop. When I got there, the pond had been fenced and cleaned into something neat and new. The library was a bank. Shankar Uncle had retired; his son sold phone covers. Monday. Vandana's best friend moved to Delhi. At dinner her mother, Madhuri, asked, 'How are you feeling, beta?' 'Fine.' Vandana went to bed at eight with a headache.
Thursday. 'Fine,' again, when her mother found her awake at one in the morning. 'My chest feels heavy. And my legs are tired.' Nothing about the friend.
The next Saturday. Madhuri sat on her bed with a sheet of paper listing feeling words. 'Just point, if any fit.' Vandana stared for a long time. Then, slowly, her finger went to lonely. Then to lost. She started to cry, and it felt, strangely, like relief.

"Sabina, beti, you keep saying 'fine' to your mother. Let your Bhupen Mama tell you something.
When I was fifteen in Tezpur, my own mother asked me every evening, 'How was school?' 'Fine.' 'Why are you so quiet?' 'Nothing.' That was my whole vocabulary. And I wasn't lying. I truly didn't know what else to say.
But my body knew. My neck hurt, my stomach hurt before exams, I got headaches every Sunday night. I told the school nurse about the aches, never about anything else, because there was no anything else I could find.
Years later I realised I had been frightened of failing, and lonely after my best friend changed schools. I didn't have the words. I had a stiff neck instead.
So when you say 'fine' and then complain about your back, I don't think you're cold, Sabina. I think you're like me at fifteen."

The wedding hall in Nashik was so loud that Pradeep's ears rang. His board results had come out two days earlier, lower than anyone expected, and every relative seemed to have heard.
"Pradeep! How are you, beta?"
"Fine."
"And results?"
"Fine."
By the tenth aunty his jaw ached from smiling and his stomach felt knotted. He slipped out to the terrace, away from the band.
His cousin Chaitanya was already there, eating a samosa. "You've said 'fine' about fifty times."
"Because I am fine."
"Fine like good, or fine like nothing to say?"
Pradeep leaned on the railing. "My stomach hurts. And my head. I don't know."
"That's not nothing."
Pradeep shrugged, but he stayed. When the band started again downstairs, Chaitanya didn't go back either.

Class teacher's note, Class 10B, 14 November.
Vidya has answered "fine" to every question about how she is for three weeks. When I asked after class today, she said, "Nothing, ma'am. Just a headache." She has mentioned headaches four times this month, and a stiff neck. She sits at the edge of the group at lunch.
Her friend Deepika told me quietly that Vidya's grandfather, who lived with them, passed away in October. Vidya has not mentioned it.
She isn't cold with me. She smiles and hands in work. But when I asked if she felt sad, she looked confused, as if the word didn't fit anything she knew.
What I did next: I asked the counsellor to meet her gently, and I'll call her parents today about the headaches, so they can check with the doctor if they keep going.

Sunday
Mum asked me three times today how I'm doing. Fine. Vaibhav asked why I'm so quiet. Nothing.
I don't know what else to say. I'm not sad exactly. My shoulders hurt all the time. My jaw is tight when I wake up. I can't eat breakfast.
Since we moved, everything is okay, I think. New school is okay. I just don't feel anything I can name. It's like there's a fog.
Mum said, "Beta, you can tell me if something's wrong." I said, "Nothing's wrong." And then I went to my room and my chest felt heavy for an hour.
I wish there was a word for this.
– Gurleen

"What's wrong?"
"Nothing."
"You didn't eat."
"Stomach hurts." It had hurt for a week. So had his back. He told the school nurse his head ached. He told his mother he was fine.
Alka tried again on the walk home. "Are you angry about the crop? Sad? Scared about fees?"
Yogesh kicked a stone. "I don't know. I'm just... fine. My body is tired." He wasn't cold to her; he walked closer to her than usual, and he waited when she stopped to buy peanuts. He just had no words for whatever sat in his chest.
That night Alka told their mother about the week of stomach aches.

Lucia's parents had been fighting since the spring. At dinner in their flat in Valencia, her uncle Siddiq, visiting from Manchester, asked, "How's school, how are you feeling about everything?"
"Fine."
"Just fine?"
"Yeah. Nothing's wrong." She pushed rice around the plate. "My neck's a bit stiff."
Later he found her on the balcony, rubbing her shoulders. "Stiff neck again?"
"And headache. I'm always tired."
"Lucia, if you had a word for what's in there, what would it be?"
She shrugged, not rudely. She leaned against him the way she had when she was small. "I don't know. I really don't. It's just heavy."
"Heavy is a good word to start with," he said, and stayed.

In the girls' hostel of the Pune junior college, the warden, Jyoti Ma'am, had seen Lavanya at the medical room three times that month.
"Headache again?"
"Yes, Ma'am. And stomach."
"Anything bothering you? Studies? Home?"
"No, Ma'am. I'm fine. Nothing."
Jyoti Ma'am had also seen Lavanya helping the younger girls with their hair every morning, laughing with them at breakfast. She was not a cold child. But whenever anyone asked how she felt, she answered only with her body.
Jyoti Ma'am pulled out a chart with feeling words and pictures. "Just point, if anything's close."
Lavanya looked for a long time. Her finger drifted to "homesick", then to "worried". "Maybe both? I don't know. I don't know how to say it."

Tanvir, you asked on the podcast about the year I kept saying 'fine'. I'm Ishita. When I was fifteen, everyone asked me how I was: my mother, my teachers, my friends. I always said 'fine' or 'nothing'. I wasn't lying. I truly didn't know what to call it. What I did know was that my shoulders hurt, my stomach churned before school, and I got headaches every evening. I told my mother about the headaches, and she took me to the doctor. The doctor checked me and asked, 'Is anything worrying you?' I said, 'No.' Then she handed me a sheet with feeling words and said, 'Circle any that fit.' I circled 'anxious', 'lonely' and 'overwhelmed'. I was stunned. They'd been there all along. I just hadn't had the map.

Kunal, you keep saying "fine" when your mother asks. Let me tell you about the Losar I turned fifteen, in Dharamshala. Pala had gone to work in Delhi that winter. Everyone kept asking, "Tenzin, what's wrong?" and I always said, "Nothing. Fine." I really believed it. What I did have was headaches, and a stomach that hurt before every meal. Amala took me to the clinic twice; the doctor found nothing. At the Losar feast I sat with my momos going cold, rubbing my forehead. Then my teacher asked a different question: "If that headache could talk, what would it say?" I was quiet a long time. "It would say Pala isn't here." That was the first time I had words for it.

He tried again, frowning, tongue out. Nirmala sat back and waited. At last both were on, back to front. He stamped proudly. Then a dog barked at the gate, and Tenzin ran straight into her lap and held on tight.

At the door of their flat in Toronto, Esther held out Abebe's snowsuit. "Arms in, sweetheart." "Me!" Abebe, not quite three, grabbed the zip and tugged. It stuck. He tugged harder and roared. Esther's hand hovered. Then she held the bottom of the zip steady and let him pull. Up it went. "I did it!" He marched to the door alone. Outside, the wind hit his face, and he turned and lifted both arms. "Mama, carry."

I'm Darshan, and I only noticed this last term. In the hostel mess in Pune, when someone made a joke about Biharis, I suddenly felt very Bihari, more than I ever had back in Patna. In the lab, arguing about circuit design with Madhav, I was just an engineer; nobody's hometown mattered. And on match nights in the common room I was a cricket fan, yelling at the screen with guys from five states. Same week, same me, three different people stepping forward depending on who was around. At first it confused me. Then it helped. When the mess joke stung, I remembered I'm also the boy who fixed Madhav's circuit, and the one who predicted Sunday's batting collapse. No single label gets to be the whole of me.

College counsellor's note, 7 March. Written by Kashish. Avni, nineteen, came in upset after a clash in a group project. She said that in her Gujarati cultural society she is proud to be "the Gujarati girl," in the coding club she is "the only woman who stays till midnight," and in her dance troupe she is just "Avni who's always late." This week a teammate joked that girls in tech "just do the slides," and for two days she felt like nothing but the woman in tech, and angry. We talked about how different parts of her take the lead depending on the company she is in. She listed five identities on a sheet. By the end she was smiling a little: "He saw one of me. There are four others." She will raise the remark with the project lead, and see me again next week.

Sunday night. Strange week. At the Delhi university welcome, everyone called me "the Bombay girl," and I found myself defending vada pav as if my life depended on it. Tuesday, in the philosophy seminar, I was only a student fighting about free will; nobody cared where I came from. Thursday, at the badminton club, I was just Ira who smashes too hard. Friday, Pema from Gangtok and I sat on the hostel roof, and she said she felt more "northeastern" in Delhi than she ever did at home. Me too, with Mumbai. It's as if different parts of me step forward depending on who's in the room. Honestly, that's a relief. If someone squeezes me down to "the Bombay girl," I know it's one door, not the house. - Ira
