In the village near Akola, everyone called Vaibhav "the college boy" the moment he got off the bus with his agriculture textbooks. Uncles asked him about seed prices as if he were an expert. At the college hostel, it was the opposite: there he was "the farmer's son," the one who knew how cotton actually smelled. And on Sunday evenings at the kabaddi ground, he was simply the best raider on the left side. His cousin Indu laughed when he told her. "So which one are you?" Vaibhav, twenty, thought about it while they walked back past the well. "All of them. Whichever the people around me notice first." Last month a hostel senior had sneered at "village boys," and it had stung for days. Now it stung less. He was a village boy. He was also three other things.

In her first year at university in Madrid, Dalia noticed she changed depending on the room. At home with her grandmother, she was Moroccan: mint tea, Darija, Friday couscous. In the engineering lab she was just an engineer, arguing about load calculations with Tariq and forgetting everything else. On Saturday nights at the stadium, she was a football fan, screaming with strangers who didn't care where she came from. It confused her at first. Then one afternoon a classmate made a joke about "people like her," and suddenly she felt only Moroccan, hot-faced and defensive. The next morning, in the lab, Tariq asked her about the bridge model, and she was an engineer again. Walking home, she realised no single label had the whole of her. That felt freeing.

In the hostel at the engineering college in Warangal, Simran, nineteen, from Patna, was surprised how often she changed. At dinner, when a group mocked Bihari accents, she felt fiercely Bihari, suddenly using words she hadn't said in months. In the robotics lab, nobody cared where she came from; she was the one who knew the motor controls, and she felt like pure engineer. At the common-room TV on match nights, she was a cricket fan, yelling next to Mahima from Kerala. "You're like three different people," Mahima said. Simran laughed. "Maybe that's good." She thought of how, a year ago, she'd felt only Bihari, only an outsider, every day. Now, depending on who was in the room, other parts of her came forward. No single label could swallow her.

You asked if Bengaluru has changed me. I think it's more that I've split into pieces, in a good way.
At the Tamil landlord's house, I'm "the Odia boy." At work in the start-up, I'm the backend guy, and nobody cares what I eat. On Sundays I play cricket in the park with a group from Kerala and Assam, and I'm just the slow bowler who can't field.

Realising this helps. No single label gets to be all of me. Tell Maa I'm eating properly.
Harish

Counsellor's note, Thursday. Observed at the athletics ground, 4 p.m.
Coach asked Keerthana (15, squad captain) to run a punishment set for Sahil, who had missed Monday's practice. First it was 'two laps, just to remind him.' Keerthana blew the whistle and held the stopwatch. After the second lap Sahil was bent over, and she looked at Coach. Coach said calmly, 'Continue. It's for his own good.' Keerthana's hands were shaking on the stopwatch. She said 'one more' four more times, each time quieter, glancing back at Coach instead of at Sahil.
At that point I walked over, stopped the set and sat Sahil in the shade with water. Keerthana burst into tears: 'I didn't know how to stop once I'd started.' I spoke with her afterwards and raised the punishment laps with the head of sports the same afternoon.

Sunday night. I feel sick. Lakshmi-di is directing the annual play and I'm her assistant, so on Wednesday when she said, 'Move Rupa to the back row, she's too short for the front,' I did it. Easy. The scout camp was held in the school ground of a kasba outside Satara. Ganga, fifteen, was patrol leader, and the senior troop leader, Farhan, was a college boy everyone admired. When little Sonu came to assembly without his scarf, Farhan said calmly, "Ganga, make him stand in the sun. Just five minutes." Ganga did. Five minutes later: "Now arms out. Just continue." She hesitated. Sonu's lips were dry. "It's discipline. Continue," Farhan said, without raising his voice. Ganga's throat was tight, and her voice shook as she said, "Arms up, Sonu." Stopping now felt like saying the first five minutes were wrong. Then the scout teacher, Mrs Pillai, crossed the ground, stopped it at once, sat Sonu in the shade with water, and spoke to Farhan and Ganga separately before lunch.

The school newspaper office in Accra smelled of printer ink. Yara, sixteen, had just joined as a junior editor, and Nkechi, the senior editor, was someone she wanted to impress. Friday: Anirban didn't come to practice. Yamini sat on the bench, knees pulled up, feeling sick. After practice she went to the school counsellor and told her everything. The counsellor listened, said she'd speak to the sports head that day, and asked Yamini to walk with her to find Anirban.

The NCC camp ground in Nagpur was baking at forty degrees. Senior cadet Baljeet told Kavitha, sixteen, "Make the juniors hold the plank. Two minutes. Discipline." Kavitha felt uneasy but nodded; it was only two minutes. When the time was up, Baljeet said calmly, "Another two." Some juniors were shaking. Kavitha's heart was racing. "Continue," Baljeet said, without raising his voice. She counted again, hating her own voice. One small boy's arms buckled and he lay face-down, gasping. Kavitha's legs moved before she thought. She knelt, helped him sit up in the shade, and handed him her water bottle. "Enough," she said to Baljeet, shaking. Then she ran to the officer's tent and reported it. The officer came out at once, ended the drill, and had the boy checked by the camp medic.

It was the kabaddi team's evening practice in a Nagpur school ground. Tushar, the senior captain, pointed at the new junior who had come late. "Pankaj, make him do ten push-ups."
Ten was fine. Pankaj counted.
"Now twenty. Discipline matters." Tushar's voice was calm, almost bored.
The junior's arms were shaking. Pankaj's throat went tight. Should he say something? But he had already done ten. Stopping now would look like saying Tushar was wrong.
"Keep going," Tushar said. "Just continue."
Pankaj kept counting. His voice cracked on fourteen. The junior's face was red and wet.

Assistant coach's note, athletics camp, day 3.
The head coach told Sharmila, the team captain, to 'toughen up' Kiran, a new runner who had missed a morning session. It started small: one extra lap. Then another. The coach kept saying, calmly, 'Continue. It's for his own good.' By the sixth lap in the midday heat, Kiran was stumbling and asking to stop. Sharmila was biting her lip, glancing at the coach, clearly upset, but she kept counting laps aloud. When I stepped in and stopped it, she burst into tears. 'I wanted to stop after two. But he was so calm, and I'd already done two.' I sat Kiran in the shade with water and called the camp doctor. I reported the instruction to the camp director immediately, and spoke with Sharmila, who asked to talk to Kiran herself tonight.

Thursday night.
I can't stop thinking about rehearsal. Rhea Didi, the drama club head, told me to 'coach' Asif from Class 8 because he stammers on his lines. First she said, make him repeat the line. Fine. Then, make him say it louder. Then, in front of everyone, make him say it until he gets it right. Asif's face went red, then white. If I say nothing, Kasper will get bored, Omar told himself on the walk to school in Rotterdam. Nobody wants to be boring, not even a bully. On Monday Kasper flicked his ear and called him "Sleepy Omar". Omar kept his eyes on his sums and said nothing. On Tuesday Kasper gave up after two tries and went back to his lunch. Omar felt lighter, almost proud. But by Friday the nickname had reached two other boys, and Kasper was back, louder, waiting at the gate. Omar's stomach tightened. Maybe saying nothing was only the first step, he thought, and not the whole plan.

Every day at the water cooler, two boys from 7B called Jomon a name about his accent and knocked his bottle aside. Jomon had a plan. "If I don't react, they'll get bored." So he stared at the wall and filled his bottle slowly. On Monday they said it twice and left. On Tuesday, only once. He thought it was working. On Thursday they followed him to the stairs, saying it louder, and one of them yanked his bag strap. His classmate Charu saw it. "Ignoring isn't stopping them," Charu said. Jomon's jaw was tight. "It worked a bit." "A bit isn't enough. Come on." They went to the class teacher before the next period. She listened to both of them, told Jomon it was not his fault, and walked straight to the 7B teacher's room while they waited.

I'm Arnab, and I sit behind Lalit. Since the start of term, a boy in our class has been flicking Lalit's ear in the line and calling him "Specs". Lalit told me his plan was to act as if he couldn't hear. For a week it sort of worked: the boy did it less, maybe got bored. Then on Monday he snatched Lalit's glasses and passed them round the room. Lalit still said nothing. His hands were shaking when he got them back. Voice note for Rafiq, from Pema. You asked how I even started on boards, so listen. First I counted backwards starting from the exam in March, week by week, and found I had about twenty-two weeks. Then I wrote every chapter of every subject beside its marks weightage from the syllabus sheet. That was the surprise: Science and Maths hold the biggest share, and I had been giving Social Science the same time as them. So I gave the early mornings, when my head is clear, to Science and Maths, and moved the lighter subjects to the evening. I taped the count of weeks over my desk. It does not make the syllabus smaller, but now it looks like a mountain with steps.

Jyoti shares a desk lamp with me in the hostel, so I watched the whole thing on Sunday night. She pinned a calendar to the wall and drew a red circle round 3 March, the first board paper. Then she counted backward, tapping each week with her pen. "Sixteen weeks. Not twenty. Sixteen." Next she opened the marking scheme and wrote every chapter on a sheet with its marks beside it. Nazia from the next room leaned in the doorway and asked why she was wasting time on lists. "Look," Jyoti said, pointing. "Science and Maths have the heaviest chapters, and they're my weakest. English I'm fine in. So those two get my mornings, five to seven, when my head's clear." I don't know if she was calm or scared underneath. Her pen was very steady, though. On Monday her alarm went off at a quarter to five.

10 November. Manoj sits on the floor with the exam datesheet and a school calendar. Maths on 11 March, Science on 18 March. He counts the weeks back from the first paper. Seventeen. Then he goes through the syllabus and writes the marks for each chapter beside it. "Chemistry and algebra carry the most, and they're my worst," he mutters. Leela Chechi, home from college, watches him circle them. "Then those get the mornings," he decides. "Five-thirty to seven-thirty, before anyone's awake."
14 January. The mornings are still dark and he still hates the alarm. But the chemistry chapter on acids is done, and he can explain it to Leela Chechi without the book.
11 March. Before leaving for the Maths paper he turns the plan over. On the back, in November handwriting: "Hardest first. Most marks first."

Rehana, you say you spend an hour every evening deciding when to start? Let me tell you about my board year. I am Nikita, and I used to negotiate with myself daily: after a snack, after a call, after the news. The evening would slip away. Then one night I wrote a single sentence on a card: "When it is six o'clock and my chai is finished, I open Physics at my desk." No decision, only a cue. The first evening felt silly. By the third, my hand reached for the book as soon as the cup was empty, and Amma, who brought the chai, found it already open when she came to collect the cup. The debate ended because there was nothing left to debate.

Every evening Usha lost an hour to the same argument with herself: start now, or after one more video, or after Ojas stops practising the harmonium in the next room. The Physics paper was five weeks away and the argument was winning. On Sunday she sat on the balcony and thought it through. Deciding every day is the problem. So I decide once. If it's six and my chai cup is empty, Physics comes out on the desk. Harmonium or no harmonium. She wrote it on a sticky note and stuck it on the cup. On Monday at six, Ojas was halfway through a very loud scale. Usha drained her chai, looked at the note and walked to the desk. Earplugs in. Book open, chapter four. No debate.

For two weeks, Ramesh's evenings in the Hyderabad flat went the same way. Home at half past five, chai, phone, then "I'll start at seven." Seven became eight. Eight became dinner. On Friday he caught himself arguing with the clock again and stopped. The deciding is what eats the time, he thought. What if I don't decide? What if the chai decides? He tore a page from his rough book and wrote: When it's 6 and the chai is finished, Physics opens at the desk. He stuck it on the fridge, where Pallavi Akka read it aloud in a newsreader voice. Saturday, 6:02, cup empty. He felt the old five-more-minutes tug, looked at the fridge and went. By 6:05 he was drawing ray diagrams.

In their small town in Maharashtra, all six of Ojas's friends were taking Science, so Ojas ticked the Science box too, without much thought. On the first day Santosh cracked a joke on the back bench, and it felt like the best decision of his life. But by the first term's Physics test the picture looked different. Ojas stared at a page of forces and lenses that the other boys followed easily. His marks came in low. Friends still gathered at the tea stall after school, and being there felt warm, but Santosh was at ease with Physics and Ojas was not. "Do you actually like it?" Santosh asked once. Ojas did not know what to say.

When the subject-choice forms went round at Xin's school in Singapore, his whole lunch table ticked the triple-science box. Yuki didn't even look up. "Obviously science, right? We'll all be in the same class." Xin had spent the holidays drawing comics and helping his aunt at her design studio, but he ticked the box too. The first term was fun: the same jokes, the same table, lab partners who were already friends. Then Physics moved on to kinematics. The others groaned and got it. Xin stared at the equations until they blurred. By the second term he was copying Yuki's homework and staying back for extra classes alone, while his friends went for bubble tea without him. Being together, he realised, hadn't made the Physics any easier.

The Physics lab at Harish's school in Bhopal smelled of dust and metal, and on the last day of the first term Ira Ma'am handed back the test papers. Harish got 19 out of 70. Around him, Nikhil, Sameer and Aditya, the friends he had followed into Science because none of them could imagine separate classes, were comparing scores in the fifties. His friends were waiting outside, laughing about something, and for once he didn't want to join them.

Keerthana, your Tanuja Nani once had a brother, Mahesh, and this is about the canal. He was fifteen. After the first rains, four friends said, "Let's swim at the sluice." Mahesh's stomach turned over, because he had seen how the water ran that week. But everyone was nodding and Ravi was already pulling off his shirt, so Mahesh thought, they all want this, and said nothing. Three of them, one belief nobody had checked.

On the second night of the school trip to Nainital, someone whispered that the whole dorm was sneaking out to the lake at midnight. Siddharth lay on his bunk, stomach tight. At the back of a classroom in Madrid, Dalia watched three girls copy Ines's way of speaking and giggle whenever Ines answered a question. It had been going on for weeks. Dear Lakshmi Masi, you asked me what an ordinary Tuesday should look like in ten years, so I tried it. I want to wake up in a city of medium size like Nagpur, within a bus ride of Mummy and Papa, and sometimes work from home. Then I worked backwards: which jobs make that likely? Teaching, accounts in a local firm, software services with a nearby office. And which push it far away? Jobs that post you anywhere in the country, or firms in big cities with twelve-hour days. I've made a chart: field, qualification, cities where the work exists. Software and accounts look promising, and I can ask your friend at the software firm what it is really like. I'd been asking what to be. Now I'm asking how to live. — Vikas

Ridhi set two sheets on the staffroom table: three years of cutoffs for Computer Science at the state college, and her own mock scores. “Sir, be honest. Is this branch realistic?”

So it's not a mountain. It's 160 rupees, 26 times.

In the reading room of a university library in Prague, Marek spread five years of entrance papers across the table. Jana, studying beside him, watched him draw a chart with a ruler. "What is this?" she asked. "Every question, sorted by topic," he said. Marek added them up. Algebra and probability together took nearly half of all marks every year. Poetry analysis, a topic he loved, was worth very little. "So the first month goes to algebra and probability," he said. "The rare topics wait." Jana raised an eyebrow. "You love poetry." "I know," said Marek, "and I will get to it. Just not first." He wrote "Week 1: algebra" at the top of a fresh page.

In the coaching institute's library, Disha had spread out ten years of past papers for the state engineering services exam. Lalit, from her batch, pulled up a chair. "Past papers already? We haven't finished the syllabus." "I'm not solving them," she said. "I'm counting." She had made a table: every question, its topic, its marks. Some topics came up every single year: strength of materials, fluid mechanics, surveying. Together they were almost forty percent of the marks. Others, like one small chapter on tunnel ventilation, had appeared twice in ten years. Lalit looked at her study order. "So you're starting with the big three." "And leaving the rare ones for the last month. If I run out of time, I lose two marks, not forty." Lalit took a photo of her table. "Can I copy the method?"

Subject: my new study order (weightage!)
Hi Pallavi,
Remember how I was studying the bank exam syllabus in textbook order? I stopped this week.
I went through the last six years of papers and counted marks by topic. Reasoning was 35 of the 100 prelims marks every year. Quant was 35 too, but mostly data interpretation and arithmetic; probability and permutations together were only about 3 marks a year. English was 30, and reading comprehension was the biggest part.
So my new order: data interpretation and arithmetic first, then puzzles and seating arrangements, then reading comprehension. Probability and the rare stuff go to the last two weeks.
I feel strangely lighter. Before this, I was spending a whole week on permutations, which might give me 2 marks.
Want me to share the tally sheet?
Mahima

Farida, you asked how our Pondicherry trip didn't turn into a money fight. It's Lakshmi speaking, and honestly, it came down to one conversation.
Before booking, I said to the group, 'Let's be honest about budgets, no shame.' Meena said, a bit quietly, that her allowance is smaller and the sea-view rooms were too much for her. For a second it was awkward. Then Suhana said, 'Fine, so you take the garden-side room. It's cheaper, and it's still the same guesthouse.'
Then we split things into two kinds. Rooms: each pays their own, so Meena pays less. Shared stuff, cabs, the scooter rental, the big dinner: split equally, four ways. We wrote it in the group chat so it was clear.
On the trip, nobody looked at Meena funny at checkout. And later she told me she'd almost not come, before that talk.

I'm Mohit, and I was on the Coorg trip where Aditi quietly fixed a problem the rest of us hadn't noticed.
We'd found a homestay with two kinds of rooms: big ones with a balcony, and a smaller one at the back for less. Aditi asked everyone to fill in a short form: 'What can you spend on the room, honestly? Only I'll see it.' That evening she said, 'Okay, Farzana takes the back room, it suits her. The jeep, the coffee-estate tour and meals, we split equally.'
Farzana went a bit pink, then smiled. Nobody asked why.
At the end, the jeep bill came to 4,800, and Aditi divided it by six: 800 each, the same for everyone. I watched Farzana pay her share without hesitating. I don't know what she felt inside, but she laughed more on that trip than I'd seen all year.

In Lagos, Chidi's first pay landed on a Friday, and by Saturday it felt like a fortune. Chidi bought the newest phone on monthly instalments, ate out with colleagues four nights a week, and ordered a new jacket online. Nobody had said no, and no total had ever been set for the month. Sami, sharing the flat, saw the bags arriving and said nothing at first. By the twentieth, the account was empty. Chidi borrowed from Sami for the bus fare and skipped the office lunch. The phone instalment was due in five days. Sitting on the kitchen floor, Chidi wrote the same question three times on a napkin: where did it all go?

On the 20th, at the office cafeteria, Jasleen's card was declined for a ₹90 thali. She tried again. Declined.
Irawati, who sat next to her in the claims team, paid without comment. Back at their desks, Jasleen opened her bank app and scrolled up. The ₹32,000 salary had come on the 1st. On the 2nd, the new phone: ₹3,800 EMI for twelve months. Then the team dinner, the weekend café, delivery almost every night because the office ran late, a new jacket, a cab whenever it rained. No single thing had felt big.
'I never actually decided how much I'd spend,' she said quietly. 'I just thought it was a lot.'
Irawati nodded. 'Ten days till next salary?'
'Eleven.' Jasleen closed the app. The phone EMI would come out again on the 2nd, whether she ate or not.

I'm Binita, from the flat next door in Guwahati. Last Sunday I heard a lot of thumping and giggling through the wall, so I went over.
Chandrika had her two little brothers crouched under the heavy dining table. "Drop," she said, and they dropped. "Cover," and they tucked in, arms over their heads. "Hold on," and they grabbed the table legs.
She explained to them out loud, and I think partly to herself: "When the shaking starts, your legs want to run. But running is when things fall on you. So we practise until your body goes to the table without thinking."
Then she walked them to the door and the stairwell, pointing. "Not the lift. Never the lift."
They did it five times. By the fifth, the younger one was under the table before she'd finished saying "drop". I can't know what she was feeling, but she looked serious and satisfied.

Saturday. After the small tremor on Thursday, Shalini noticed what she and her little brother Asif had done: they had both run into the corridor. She sat down with a notebook. "Running felt right, but the corridor has the glass cabinet. What should we have done?"
She looked it up in the district disaster guide: get down low, get under something sturdy, and grip it until the shaking stops. Then she walked through the flat, looking for sturdy cover. The study desk, yes. The glass cabinet side, never. The exit: the stairs, not the lift.
Sunday. She practised with Asif. "Drop." He flopped on the floor. "Cover." He crawled under the desk. "Hold on." He grabbed the leg. They did it in his room, the kitchen and the living room.
The next Wednesday. Asif's school ran a surprise drill. That evening he announced, "I was first under the table. My teacher said well done." Shalini just smiled.

I sat next to Nikita at the insurance firm for three years, so I saw her plan from the outside.
When Farid was promoted to manager in just two years, Nikita wrote his steps on a sticky note: lead a migration, present at the all-hands, get a certification. She pinned it to her screen.
She did all three within eighteen months. I watched her present at the all-hands, hands shaking a bit but clear.
What neither of us thought about was that Farid had been in the digital team, which doubled in size that year and needed new managers. Nikita's claims team hadn't grown at all. There was simply no manager seat.
When the promotion list came out without her name, she took the sticky note down and folded it very small. I don't know exactly what she felt. She didn't talk much that week.
— Vinita

Week one. At lunch, Darshan told Nikita how he'd become team lead in two years: run the internal tools project, mentor two juniors, speak at the tech meet. Nikita wrote it on a napkin. If it worked for him, it would work for her.
Month six. Nikita had finished all three. She was proud of the tech-meet talk. But her manager kept saying, "There's no lead opening on our side right now." Darshan's team had doubled that year; hers had lost two people.
Month ten. The promotion list came out. Darshan's teammate got the new lead role. Nikita wasn't on it.
She found the napkin in her drawer and stared at it. Same steps, different team. She felt foolish for not asking the obvious question at the start: was there even a seat?

On the terrace above his family's hardware shop in a small town, Chetan had one online post open on his phone: a stranger claiming to have joined a company at triple the usual pay. Phone to his ear, he told Nirmala, the recruiter, "I want three times what you offered." A pause. "That's far beyond what this role pays," Nirmala said politely. "I don't think we can go further. Thank you for your time." The line clicked. Chetan sat among the rolls of wire, stunned. Two weeks passed and no other offer came. He wrote back to ask about the original figure. Nirmala replied that the role was still open, but the number on offer now was lower than before.

Tuan had read a post on a forum the night before: someone claimed a junior data engineer in Melbourne had been offered almost double the usual pay. He screenshotted it.
The next afternoon, on a video call, Wanjiru, the start-up's recruiter, asked about his expectations.
"I'm looking for this," Tuan said, naming a figure far above what the job ad had suggested.
Wanjiru's eyebrows rose. "That's well above the market for this level. Where does that number come from?"
"I've seen it offered elsewhere."
"I see." She was polite, but her voice cooled. "I don't think we're a match on this. Thank you for your time."
The call ended in under ten minutes.
A week later, after two other rejections, Tuan emailed Wanjiru to ask if the role was still open at the advertised range. He felt his cheeks burn as he pressed send.

The HR office at the private hospital in Siliguri had a calendar of the Kanchenjunga and a fan that wobbled.
Dolma, the HR manager, smiled at Jomon. "We'd like you as our biomedical technician. This is the offer."
Jomon didn't look at the paper. He'd seen a post in a group chat the night before: someone claiming a technician in a big city hospital got triple the normal pay.

Pradeep, you look tense about your guest list, so let me tell you what my husband and I did. I'm Shreya. Ten days before our wedding we had a shouting match over it. He wanted a hundred and fifty, and I had found myself adding relatives until it reached three hundred. I walked out to the balcony, he stayed in the kitchen, and we didn't speak for an hour. Then he came out with two cups of tea. We set a cap of two hundred and a rule: exceptions only if both of us agreed. The next day we sat with each set of parents and said why: "We want to be able to talk with everyone who comes." Both families listened. It hurt less because we had both stopped fighting first.

The printer needed the final guest count by nine the next morning, and at eleven at night Ishita and Karthik were shouting in the kitchen of their rented flat in Chennai.
"Your mother added thirty people today!"
"And your father added twenty-five last week!"
Karthik slammed the list on the counter. Then he stopped, breathed, and sat on the floor. "This isn't working."
Ishita sat next to him. After a minute, she said, "Let's stop adding and make a rule. A cap. Two hundred and fifty."
"And exceptions?"
"Only if someone we'd actually miss would be hurt. And we both have to agree."
They went through the list quietly with that rule. Two exceptions made it; the rest didn't.
At midnight they wrote a message to both families: the cap, the reason (the hall size and their savings), and a thank-you. They sent the count to the printer at eight fifty.

The fight in Darshan and Aditi's Pune flat started over a single name: Aditi's college friend's parents.
"You don't even know them!"
"You invited your entire gym!"
Aditi grabbed her keys and walked out. Darshan sat on the balcony, angry and then just tired.
She came back an hour later with two cups of tea. "Can we start again? Not the list. The rules."
They agreed on a cap of two hundred. Then an exception rule: someone outside the cap comes only if both of them would feel the person's absence at the wedding. Not one, both.
Suddenly the list was easy. The gym friends were out. The college friend's parents, who had once fed Aditi for a month, were in; Darshan agreed after hearing that.
On Sunday they visited both sets of parents together and explained: the cap, the rule, the reasons. Aditi's mother grumbled, then said, "Fine. At least it's fair."

On Monday, Uday told the team on the group thread, "I'm offline after six from now on." He got three thumbs-up. By Friday Uday was back to replying at nine at night, and felt worse than before, because he had said out loud that he would not.

On Monday morning Uday pulled the whole team into the meeting room and put a sheet on the wall screen: monthly users, money in, money out, the line going the wrong way. "This is where we are," Uday said. "And this is what I want to change."
Then came a slide for each role. The mobile work would move to a clinic dashboard. Support and sales would sit together. Parul's testing job would grow to cover the new product, with the same pay and a new review date.
Parul asked three questions about the numbers and then nodded slowly. By Friday two engineers had handed in notice, politely and with thanks. Everyone else stayed and started on Monday's plan.

"Everyone, I'll show you the numbers first, then the decision." Damini clicked to the chart. "Four hundred schools tried the parent app. Eleven pay. But sixty schools use the fee-reminder feature every week, and forty have asked if they can pay for just that."
"So we're switching to fee reminders?" Rehana asked from the back.

I put off the all-hands for two weeks because I was scared of the faces. When I finally stood in front of our nine people, Urmila, my co-founder, stood next to me with the laptop.
I didn't soften it. I showed them the churn chart for the consumer app, down every month since October, and then the one bright line: small shops using our stock alerts every single day. I said, "We're pivoting to stock alerts for shops. Here's why." Then I went role by role. Support would stay. Two marketing roles would end, with six weeks' notice and help finding new jobs. Engineering would shrink the app to one feature.
Two engineers left within a month; one told me they'd hoped to build consumer products. The other seven stayed. Later Urmila told me what one of them said in the kitchen: "I don't love it, but I can see the reasoning. That's enough."

Shalini, you asked why the product I manage feels like a mess. I can tell you exactly. It's me. I'm Uma, and for a year I gave in to whoever shouted loudest. The sales director would storm into my cabin, and I'd move his feature to the top. Then the CEO's office would call, and I'd move theirs. I'm Farida, and I've been on Sahil's team for two years. He's kind, and he hates conflict. I noticed it most in planning meetings. On Wednesday the fest committee chalked out the stalls in the school hall. Minakshi, who was running the lemonade and snacks stall, sat on the steps with Vasudha and a notebook. 'Imagine it's Saturday night and the stall flopped,' she said. 'Why?' Vasudha added, 'And the sugar might run out by noon.' Minakshi hadn't thought of any of that when she had only planned how many people would come. 2. It rained, and paper bookmarks got soggy.
3. We got the corner behind the stage where nobody walks.

So, Jagdish, you want to know why our ring-toss stall made the most money at the fest? It's because of one weird evening, the Wednesday before.
Your friend Arnab was sitting on the terrace feeling great about the plan. Rings, bottles, prizes, done. Then I thought, okay, imagine it's over and it was a disaster. Why?
First thing that came: nobody has change. Everyone brings a hundred, tickets are ten. Second: what if it rains, the bottles are on the open ground. Third: they'd put us near the toilets last year.
I actually laughed, because all three were so obvious and none were on my list.

Dear Ojas,
Thank you for testing the first version of the tuition-finder app last month. Your honest feedback, "I don't see why I'd use this," stung, but you were right.
I went back to why I started. Parents in our colony don't just want a list of tutors. They want to know which tutors other parents trust. That was the whole idea, and I'd left it out to launch faster.
So I added one thing: short reviews from parents whose children have studied with each tutor. Nothing else.
Since then I've been working in two-week cycles. At the end of each, I look at what people actually did, not what they said. In the first cycle, most people read reviews but never messaged a tutor. So I added a "call" button. Messages doubled in the second cycle.
Would you try it again?
Vinita

Ganga, you asked on the show how our library-sharing app survived its first flop. So here's Aadhya's honest version.
Our first release was just a list of books people owned. Nobody used it. The feedback was polite and useless: "Nice idea," "Looks clean."
I sat with my co-founder and asked, why did we start this? Because people in apartment buildings have shelves full of books they've read once, and neighbours who'd love to borrow them. The magic was the borrowing, and the first version didn't even let you ask to borrow.
So we added one button: "Ask to borrow." Then we worked in two-week sprints. At the end of each, we looked only at what people did. First sprint, people asked but never returned books. So we added a gentle return reminder. Second sprint, returns went up.
We stopped guessing, and started watching.

Two-year-old Hafsa toddled behind her grandfather on the veranda and picked up the spoon he had dropped, holding it up with both hands. Nobody had asked her to, and she wanted no biscuit for it.
